Global dynamics below excited solitons for the non-radial NLS with potential
Satoshi Masaki, Jason Murphy, Jun-ichi Segata

Abstract
. We consider the global dynamics of solutions to the 3 ​ d 3d cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation in the presence of an external potential, in the setting in which the equation admits both ground state solitons and excited solitons at small mass. We prove that small mass solutions with energy below that of the excited solitons either scatter to the ground states or grow their H 1 H^{1} -norm in time. In particular, we give an extension of the result of Nakanishi [ 19 ] from the radial to the non-radial setting.



1.
Introduction

We consider the following cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) in three space dimensions:

\begin{cases}i{\partial}_{t}u+Hu=\sigma|u|^{2}u,\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
H=-\Delta+V
for some external potential
V:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
, and the sign
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
gives the
focusing
and
defocusing
equations, respectively. We consider sufficiently smooth and decaying potentials supporting a single bound state. In this setting, one finds that (
1.1
) supports small solitary waves (ground states), along with excited solitons in the focusing setting.

When
V\equiv 0
and the nonlinearity is defocusing, all
H^{1}
solutions are global and scatter to free solutions as
t\to\pm\infty

[
9
]
; in the focusing case,
H^{1}
solutions with mass-energy below the ground state either scatter or blow-up in finite time
[
1
,
6
]
. In the case that
V
has negative eigenvalues, (
1.1
) has small solitary waves, leading to more complicated dynamics for solutions. When
V
has a single negative eigenvalue, solutions with sufficiently small data in
H^{1}
scatter to the family of ground states as
t\to\pm\infty

[
10
]
(see also
[
22
,
14
,
15
,
17
,
18
]
). The case when
V
has several negative eigenvalues has also been studied in several works (see e.g.
[
5
,
21
,
24
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
28
]
), although this case is not as well-understood in general.

In
[
19
]
, Nakanishi considered the dynamics of radial solutions with small mass and with energy below that of the first excited state, in the case that
V
has a single negative eigenvalue. He proved that solutions either blow up in finite time or scatter to the family of ground states (with a criterion in terms of the initial data to distinguish these two cases). In further work, he classified the behavior of radial solutions with small mass and energy slightly above the first excited state
[
20
]
. In this paper, we prove a result analogous to the one in
[
19
]
, but in the
non-radial
setting. In particular, in the focusing case, we establish a scattering/grow-up dichotomy below the excited energy and under a small mass assumption. We also obtain scattering to the ground states for small mass and arbitrary energy in the defocusing case (under the assumption that there are no excited solitons in this setting; cf. Conjecture
2.1
).

Before we can state our main result precisely, we need to introduce some notation. First, we denote the conserved
energy
for (
1.1
) by

\mathbb{E}_{V}:=\mathbb{H}_{V}-\mathbb{G},

which is the sum of the linear and nonlinear components, namely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{V}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla\varphi(x)|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}V(x)|\varphi(x)|^{2}\,dx,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=\tfrac{\sigma}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\varphi(x)|^{4}dx.

We will write
\mathbb{H}_{0}
and
\mathbb{E}_{0}
to designate the functionals corresponding to the free case, i.e. with
V\equiv 0
. The conserved
mass
is denoted by

\mathbb{M}(\varphi):=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\varphi(x)|^{2}dx.

The precise assumptions made on the potential will be discussed in Section
1.2
below. The essential requirements are that
V
be sufficiently smooth and decaying, and that
H=-\Delta+V
has one simple, negative eigenvalue. This assumption implies that
V
has a nonzero negative part. The ground states and excited states for (
1.1
) are discussed in detail in Section
2
. The set of
H^{1}
soliton profiles will be denoted by
\mathscr{S}
. The quantity
\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)
denotes the ground state energy at mass
\mu
, with
\mathscr{S}_{0}(\mu)
denoting the set of soliton profiles with mass
\mu
and energy
\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)
. Similarly,
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
denotes the excited soliton energy at mass
\mu
, with
\mathscr{S}_{1}(\mu)
denoting the set of excited soliton profiles. We will show that

\mu\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)<\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)

(1.3)

for small
\mu
, where
Q
is the ground state for the standard cubic NLS; that is, the mass-energy level of the excited state is below that of the NLS ground state (see Theorem
2.4
). This inequality is reasonable, as
V
has a non-trivial negative part. We remark that, however, it is not clear whether or not this holds under the assumption of radial symmetry.

We may parametrize the ground state solitons as
\Phi=\Phi[z]
, where
z\in\mathbb{C}
belongs to a sufficiently small disk
D
(see Lemma
2.1
). We say that a solution
u
to (
1.1
)
scatters to
\mathscr{S}_{0}
if there exists a
C^{1}
function
z:\mathbb{R}\to D
,
z_{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}
, and
u_{\pm}\in H^{1}
such that

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\|u(t)-\Phi[z(t)]-e^{-it\Delta}u_{\pm}\|_{H^{1}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}|z(t)|=z_{\pm}.

Our main result is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists
\mu_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that the following holds:

(i)

Suppose
\sigma=1
. Let
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfy

\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\leq\mu_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{V}(u_{0})<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mathbb{M}(u_{0})),

and let
u:[T_{-},T_{+}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}
denote the maximal-lifespan solution to (
1.1
). If

\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{0})<0\quad\text{and}\quad\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}>1,

(1.4)

then
\lim_{t\to T_{\pm}}\|u(t)\|_{H^{1}}=\infty
. Otherwise,
u
is global and scatters to
\mathscr{S}_{0}
.

(ii)

Suppose
\sigma=-1
and assume Conjecture
2.1
. If
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfies
\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\leq\mu_{0}
, then
u
is global and scatters to
\mathscr{S}_{0}
.

Theorem
1.1
is a non-radial analogue of the main result in
[
19
]
. It is not a complete extension of
[
19
]
in the sense that under the condition (
1.4
), we have only established ‘grow-up’ of the
H^{1}
-norm, as opposed to finite-time blowup.

In the remainder of the introduction, we will briefly describe the strategy of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, focusing primarily on the scattering result. The overarching strategy of the proof is the same as in the work of Nakanishi
[
19
]
, who proceeded by suitably adapting the concentration-compactness approach to induction on energy (also called the ‘Kenig–Merle roadmap’). Let us therefore briefly recall Nakanishi’s argument for Theorem
1.1
in the radial setting. We will then describe the main steps needed to extend this argument to the non-radial case.

The basic idea of the argument is as follows: first, one shows that if the theorem fails, then one can construct a non-scattering solution enjoying strong compactness properties in
H^{1}
. One then derives a contradiction by showing that this solution is incompatible with some monotonicity formula satisfied by the nonlinear equation (in this case, the virial identity).

Nakanishi’s argument involved significant modifications to the scheme just described, due to the fact that one is now considering scattering to the family of ground states. In particular, one decomposes the full solution to (
1.1
) in the form
u(t)=\Phi[z(t)]+\eta(t)
and essentially seeks to establish scattering for
\xi(t):=P_{c}\eta(t)
and convergence of
|z(t)|
; here
P_{c}
is the projection away from the underlying linear eigenfunction, and we may write
\eta=R[z]\xi
for a suitable operator
R[\cdot]
(cf. Section
2
). The nonlinear evolution for
u(t)
reduces to a coupled ODE-PDE system for
(z(t),\xi(t))
. Proving scattering for
\xi
is complicated by the fact that in the nonlinear equation the underlying linear part is now time-dependent (in particular, one obtains the linearized equation around the moving ground state
\Phi[z(t)]
). In order to follow the ‘roadmap’ in this setting, Nakanishi proceeded by developing concentration-compactness tools (in particular, a linear profile decomposition) adapted to the time-dependent
linearized
equation. In what follows, we describe a slight variation of Nakanishi’s approach that aligns with the presentation in this paper; however, the fundamental ideas remain the same.

Turning to the details, under the assumption that the scattering theorem fails, one may construct a sequence of solutions
u_{n}=\Phi[z_{n}]+R[z_{n}]\xi_{n}
approaching some ‘critical’ mass-energy (below the excited energy) such that certain space-time norms of
\xi_{n}
diverge as
n\to\infty
, both forward and backward in time. The key is to prove strong
H^{1}
convergence of
u_{n}(0)
along some subsequence, for then the solution to (
1.1
) with data given by
\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}(0)
can be shown to have pre-compact orbit in
H^{1}
. To achieve this, the first step is to decompose
u_{n}(0)
in a linear profile decomposition. This entails writing
u_{n}(0)
as a sum of profiles of the form
\mathcal{L}(0,s_{n}^{j};z_{n})P_{c}\varphi^{j}
, where
\mathcal{L}(t,s;z)
is the solution operator for the linearized equation around
\Phi[z(\cdot)]
(see Definition
4.3
), plus an error whose ‘linearized evolution’ is small in
L_{t}^{\infty}L_{x}^{4}
. Under the assumption that more than one profile is present (so that convergence of
u_{n}(0)
fails), one can then develop a corresponding
nonlinear profile decomposition
for the sequence
u_{n}
, wherein one constructs scattering nonlinear solutions corresponding to each profile
\varphi^{j}
. The fact that these solutions scatter relies on the ‘criticality’ of the mass-energy of the sequence
u_{n}
. By exploiting orthogonality properties of these profiles and stability theory for the nonlinear equation, one can then deduce uniform space-time bounds for the functions
\xi_{n}
, leading to the desired contradiction. As mentioned above, with the pre-compact solution in hand, one can utilize the virial identity to obtain a contradiction and complete the proof; here the condition (
1.4
) plays a key role, as the failure of this condition helps to guarantee the coercivity of the virial identity in this setting.

In
[
19
]
, Nakanishi relies on the radial assumption in an essential way, which can be traced back to the proof of the linearized profile decomposition. The basic strategy for proving such a decomposition consists of iteratively identifying and removing ‘bubbles of concentration’ from a given bounded sequence. More precisely, these ‘bubbles’ consist of fixed profiles together with sequences of parameters, which typically include space-time translations and scales, and the profiles themselves are obtained by applying these symmetries and passing to suitable weak limits. Due to the subcritical nature of (
1.1
), no scaling parameters are needed in this setting. In the radial case, space translations may also be avoided completely, as the compactness of the embedding
H^{1}_{\text{rad}}\hookrightarrow L^{4}
allows one to obtain nontrivial weak limits directly. Thus, in the linearized profile decomposition of
[
19
]
, one must contend only with time translations. The same is then true in the nonlinear profile decomposition. This ultimately implies that once one obtains the desired non-scattering solution, the solution automatically has pre-compact orbit in
H^{1}
; in particular, one does not need to incorporate any moving spatial center to obtain compactness. Such solutions are then well-adapted to the localized virial argument.

To address the non-radial case, we must first establish a linear profile decomposition that incorporates space translations when constructing the linear profiles. A key observation in this setting is the fact that in the regime
|x|\to\infty
, the effects of both the external potential
V
and the time-dependent potentials (involving the ground states) become weak. In particular, at the level of the linear profile decomposition, we are able to utilize approximation by the linear propagator
e^{-it\Delta}
. This observation plays an important role in the nonlinear profile decomposition, as well; in particular, in the regime
|x|\to\infty
, we can employ approximation by the standard cubic NLS. Here we rely in an essential way on the inequality (
1.3
); indeed, this estimate ensures that working below the excited solitons actually puts us in the scattering region for the underlying cubic NLS (cf.
[
6
]
). In particular, this implies that nonlinear profiles in the regime
|x|\to\infty
must scatter, which ultimately means that such profiles are not present in the construction of the non-scattering solution. Consequently, the non-scattering solution once again has pre-compact orbit in
H^{1}
, without the need to incorporate a moving spatial center. This puts us in the same position as
[
19
]
, and hence the rest of the argument (i.e. the localized virial argument) follows as in that work. Thus, the primary contribution of the present work is to extend both the linear and nonlinear profile decompositions to the non-radial setting.

The observation that the effect of the potential and ground states becomes weak in the regime
|x|\to\infty
, and the fact that the non-scattering solution is consequently compact in
H^{1}
without any moving spatial center, is related to several other recent works on scattering for NLS in the presence of external potentials (see e.g.
[
13
,
12
]
). In the present setting, the underlying linear and nonlinear problems are quite a bit more complicated (e.g. due to the presence of the time-dependent ground states), and the amount of technical work needed to implement this idea in a rigorous way is rather substantial. For the details, see Sections
5
and
6
.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows:

•

In Section
1.1
, we collect some basic notation and discuss the assumptions needed on the external potential in more detail.

•

In Section
2
, we discuss the existence and properties of ground state solitons and excited solitons. We note that proving the existence of the excited solitons in the non-radial setting requires some new arguments.

•

In Section
3
, we first discuss global existence for (
1.1
). We then present the decomposition of small-mass solutions around the ground state solitons described above, deriving the coupled ODE-PDE system for
(z(t),\xi(t))
. We then turn to the scattering result in Theorem
1.1
. Taking for granted a key convergence result (Proposition
3.7
) that implies the existence of the compact non-scattering solution (Proposition
3.6
), we carry out the localized virial argument to complete the proof of scattering in Theorem
1.1
. Finally, we prove the grow-up result in Theorem
1.1
. The remainder of the paper is then dedicated to establishing Proposition
3.7
, as follows.

•

In Section
4
, we discuss the linearized equation. In particular, we collect some Strichartz and dispersive estimates for this equation, and we also establish some small-data scattering and stability results.

•

In Section
5
, we prove the linear profile decomposition adapted to the linearized equation.

•

Finally, in Section
6
, we establish the key convergence result, Proposition
3.7
, by means of the nonlinear profile decomposition described above.

1.1.
Notation and preliminaries

We write
A\lesssim B
to denote the inequality
A\leq CB
for some
C>0
. We write

(f,g)=\int\bar{f}(x)g(x)\,dx

for the standard
L^{2}
inner product. We define

\|f\|_{H^{s}_{r}}=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{r}}.

Space-time norms are denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{L_{t}^{q}L_{x}^{r}}
or
\|\cdot\|_{L_{t}^{q}H^{s}_{r}}
.

We write
Q
for the cubic NLS ground state, that is, the unique nonnegative, radial, decaying solution to
-Q+\Delta Q+Q^{3}=0
(see e.g.
[
25
, Appendix B]
).

We also define here several important functionals, including the virial functional
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}
. Recall that
\mathbb{H}_{V}
and
\mathbb{G}
were defined in (
1.2
).

Definition 1.2
.

For any
\varphi\in H^{1}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=2\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi)-3\mathbb{G}(\varphi)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\tfrac{1}{2}(x\cdot\nabla V(x))|\varphi(x)|^{2}dx,

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{V}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\varphi)+\tfrac{1}{4}\int(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)(x)|\varphi(x)|^{2}dx.

1.2.
Assumptions on the external potential

For the potential
V
, we make the following assumptions: Let
L^{\infty}_{0}
be the completion of
C_{0}^{\infty}
with respect to the supremum norm. We assume the following:

(A1)

\lim_{|x|\to\infty}|V(x)|=0
;

(A2)

V,

x\cdot\nabla V,

|x|^{2}|\nabla^{2}V|\in(L^{2}+L_{0}^{\infty})(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

(A3)

-\Delta+V
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
has only one negative eigenvalue
e_{0}<0
, which is simple;

(A4)

The wave operator
W=\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{itH}e^{it\Delta}
and its adjoint
W^{*}
are bounded on the Sobolev space
H^{k}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

for
k=1,2
and some
p>8
1
1

1

The condition (A4) is satisfied if
H
admits no eigenfunction or resonance at zero energy,
V\in L^{p}
and
\widehat{V}\in\dot{B}^{1/2}_{2,1}
(cf.
[
3
]
).
.

These are essentially the same assumptions as in
[
19
]
. Note that we do not assume radial symmetry on
V
. Note also that in
[
19
]
, the additional assumption
V/|x|\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is added to (A2), which is used in the proof of Conjecture
2.1
in the radial setting.

In addition, we suppose

(A5)

V\in H^{1}_{3/2};

(A6)

–

V
,
x\cdot\nabla V\in L^{\infty}
;

–

V
and
x\cdot\nabla V
are continuous at the origin;

–

V(0)=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}V(x)<0.

Remark 1.3
.

Assumption (A6) warrants some discussion. This assumption is used in the proof that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
obeys the bound (
1.3
) and that there exists an excited state for small mass. The assumption
V(0)=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}V(x)
is rather a characterization of the origin. If a potential
V
satisfies
V(x_{0})=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}V(x)
for some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and if
V
and
(x-x_{0})\cdot\nabla V
are continuous at
x=x_{0}
, then an application of a suitable space translation to (
1.1
) enables us to assume
x_{0}=0
. Note that the validity of all other assumptions holds under space translation. Thus, we are essentially supposing that the existence of such a point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We also remark that the inequality
\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}V(x)<0

follows from (A3). Indeed, if
V
does not have a negative part, we see that
H\geqslant 0
and there exists no negative eigenvalue.

2.
Ground states and excited states

In this section, we discuss the existence of soliton solutions to (
1.1
). We denote by
\mathscr{S}
the set of functions
\varphi\in H^{1}
satisfying

(H+\omega)\varphi=\sigma|\varphi|^{2}\varphi\quad\text{for some}\quad\omega\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.1)

In particular, for
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}
, we have that
u(t,x)=e^{-i\omega t}\varphi
is a soliton solution to (
1.1
). As we will see, solitons arise as energy minimizers. In particular, given a fixed mass
\mu>0
, we first define

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\inf\{\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)\ |\ \varphi\in\mathscr{S},\ \mathbb{M}(\varphi)=\mu\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{0}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\{\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\ |\ \varphi\in\mathscr{S},\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)=\mu,\,\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)=\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)\}.

This minimization problem will yield the ground state solitons. We also define

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\inf\{\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)\ |\ \varphi\in\mathscr{S},\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)=\mu,\,\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)>\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\{\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\ |\ \varphi\in\mathscr{S},\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)=\mu,\,\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)=\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)\}.

In the focusing case, this minimization problem will yield the excited solitons.

The existence of small solitary waves for (
1.1
) was addressed in
[
22
,
23
,
10
]
. We import the results we need here. We write
\phi_{0}
for the normalized eigenfunction of
H
associated with the unique negative eigenvalue
e_{0}
, i.e.,

H\phi_{0}=e_{0}\phi_{0},\quad\mathbb{M}(\phi_{0})=1.

Lemma 2.1
.

There exists
z_{0}>0
and a
C^{1}
-map

(\Phi,\Omega):\{z\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |z|^{2}<2z_{0}\}\to H^{1}\times\mathbb{R},

such that
(\Phi[z],\Omega[z])
satisfies

(H+\Omega[z])\Phi[z]=\sigma|\Phi[z]|^{2}\Phi[z].

(2.2)

The parameters satisfy the following:

•

(\Phi[z],\phi_{0})=z
(so that
\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}\perp\phi_{0})
,

•

\Phi[e^{i\theta}z]=e^{i\theta}\Phi[z]
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and

•

if
z
is real, then
\Phi[z]
is real-valued.

Moreover, the following hold as
|z|\to 0
:

\Phi[z]=z\phi_{0}+o(|z|^{2})\quad\text{in }H^{1},\quad\Omega[z]=-e_{0}+o(|z|)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{C}.

Finally,

\mathbb{M}(\Phi[z])=|z|^{2}+o(|z|^{4})

is an increasing bijective function of
|z|
.

The small solitons are energy minimizers for fixed small mass (see
[
22
,
23
,
10
]
):

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
z_{0}
be as in Lemma
2.1
. There exists
z_{1}\leqslant z_{0}
and
\mu_{1}>0
such that

\{\varphi\in\mathscr{S}_{0}\ |\ \mathbb{M}(\varphi)<\mu_{1}\}=\{\Phi[z]\ |\ |z|<z_{1}\}.

Moreover, if
0<\mu<\mu_{1}
then

\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{V}(\Phi[\zeta_{0}(\mu)])\sim e_{0}\mu<0,

where
\zeta_{0}
is an inverse function of
z\mapsto\mathbb{M}(\Phi[z])\in(0,\mu_{1}).

We will also need the following important ‘trichotomy’ for functions in
H^{1}
of small mass. This result appears as
[
19
, Lemma 2.3]
.

Proposition 2.3

(Trichotomy)
.

There exist
C,\mu_{3}>0
such that
if
\varphi\in H^{1}
satisfies

\mathbb{M}(\varphi)\leqslant\mu_{3}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)\leqslant C\mathbb{M}(\varphi)^{-1},

then one of the following hold:

(i)

\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi)\lesssim\mathbb{M}(\varphi)
;

(ii)

\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)\gtrsim\mathbb{M}(\varphi)
and
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)\sim\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)\sim\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi)
;

(iii)

\sigma=1
and
\mathbb{G}(\varphi)\gtrsim\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi)\gtrsim\mathbb{M}(\varphi)^{-1}
.

We turn to the existence of excited states. We consider first the focusing equation (i.e.
\sigma=1
) and work under the assumptions on
V
detailed in Section
1.2
. The analogue of the following result was established in
[
19
, Proposition 2.5]
in the radial setting. In particular, the argument in
[
19
]
relies on the radial symmetry via the compactness of the embedding
H_{\text{rad}}^{1}\hookrightarrow L^{4}
. To address the non-radial case, we instead utilize a profile decomposition

Theorem 2.4

(Excited states in the focusing case)
.

There exist

\mu_{2}\in(0,\mu_{1}]
and a constant
C\in(0,|V(0)|)

such that for
0<\mu<\mu_{2}
,
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
is continuous and decreasing in
\mu
, and

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)={}

\displaystyle\inf\{\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)\ |\ \varphi\in H^{1},\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)=\mu,\,\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)=0,\,\mathbb{G}(\varphi)\geqslant 1\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\inf\{\mathbb{I}_{V}(\varphi)\ |\ \varphi\in H^{1},\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)\leqslant\mu,\,\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)\leqslant 0,\,\mathbb{G}(\varphi)\geqslant 1\}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\mu^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)-C\mu,

(2.3)

with
\mathscr{S}_{1}(\mu)
nonempty. Moreover,
for any
c>0
there exists
\mu_{2}^{\prime}\in(0,\mu_{2}]

such that

\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)-c)<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)<\mu^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)+(V(0)+c)\mu

(2.4)

holds for
\mu\in(0,\mu_{2}^{\prime}]
.
Furthermore, there exists a continuous map

\kappa:(0,\mu_{2})\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to(0,\tfrac{1}{2})\quad\text{such that}\quad|\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)|\geqslant\kappa(\mathbb{M}(\varphi),\delta)

(2.5)

for any
\varphi\in H^{1}
satisfying

\mathbb{M}(\varphi)<\mu_{2},\quad\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)-\delta,\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert\nabla\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\geqslant 1.

Remark 2.5
.

In Nakanishi
[
19
]
, the radial version of
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)
, which we denote
\mathcal{E}_{1,\mathrm{rad}}(\mu)
, is considered. When
V
is radial, both minimization quantities make sense, and by definition, we have
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)\leqslant\mathcal{E}_{1,\mathrm{rad}}(\mu)
.
For
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)
, the estimate (
2.4
) holds. On the other hand, we do not have a similar upper bound for
\mathcal{E}_{1,\mathrm{rad}}(\mu)
; Instead, we merely have
\lim_{\mu\to 0}\mu\mathcal{E}_{1,\mathrm{rad}}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)
.

To prove the theorem, we introduce the quantity

\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu):=\inf\{\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)\ |\ \varphi\in H^{1},\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)=\mu,\,\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)=0,\,\mathbb{G}(\varphi)\geqslant 1\}.

(2.6)

In fact, we will ultimately prove that
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)={\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
.

Proof.

The identity

\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)=\inf\{\mathbb{I}_{V}(\varphi)\ |\ \varphi\in H^{1},\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)\leqslant\mu,\,\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)\leqslant 0,\,\mathbb{G}(\varphi)\geqslant 1\}

(2.7)

can be established by the same argument as in
[
19
]
. Note that the set in question is nonempty, so that
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
is finite. It also follows that
\mu\mapsto\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
is decreasing.

The rest of the proof is divided into six steps. The first four steps are devoted to the proof of the existence of a minimizer to
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
. We also establish the existence of the map
\kappa
. Most of the proof of these two statements may be done in parallel. To give a unified treatment, we introduce a parameter
E_{\infty}\in(0,\tilde{\mathscr{E}_{1}}(\mu)]
. In what follows, we will need the following inequality
for some
C>0
and
\mu_{*}>0
:

E_{\infty}\leqslant{}\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)\leqslant{\tfrac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C\mu}

(2.8)

for
\mu\in(0,\mu_{*}]
.
We will prove the inequality below (see Proposition
2.6
and observe that
\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)=\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)
).

Step 1
.
Let
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{1}
be a sequence such that

\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{n})\to E_{\infty},\quad\mathbb{M}(\varphi_{n})\to\mu,\quad\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{n})\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty,

with
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})\geqslant 1
for all
n
.
By Gagliardo–Nirenberg and Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})={}

\displaystyle 3\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{n})-\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{n})-\tfrac{1}{2}\int(x\cdot\nabla V+3V)|\varphi_{n}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle 3\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{n})-\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{n})+C\mathbb{M}(\varphi_{n})+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n}),

so that
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})\leqslant 6E_{\infty}+2C\mu+1
for large
n
; in particular,
\{\varphi_{n}\}
is bounded in
H^{1}
.

Now, as
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we deduce from
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})\geqslant 1
and the trichotomy of Proposition
2.3
that

\mu^{-1}\lesssim\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})\lesssim\mathbb{G}(\varphi_{n})

for large
n
. Furthermore, using the fact that
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we may also deduce that that
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})\sim\mathbb{G}(\varphi_{n})
by absorbing the potential term as above. In particular, we obtain boundedness of
\mathbb{G}(\varphi_{n})
. Passing to a subsequence if necessary, we may therefore assume that
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})
and
\mathbb{G}(\varphi_{n})
converge as
n\to\infty
to some limits
{H}_{\infty}
and
G_{\infty}
, which then satisfy
G_{\infty}\sim H_{\infty}\gtrsim\mu^{-1}
.

We now apply a profile decomposition to
\{\varphi_{n}\}
with remainder vanishing in
L^{4}
(see e.g.
[
11
]
). This takes the form

\varphi_{n}=u_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}T_{y_{n}^{j}}u_{j}+r_{n}^{J},

where
(T_{y}f)(x):=f(x-y)
denotes translation. In particular,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}|y_{n}^{j}-y_{n}^{k}|\to\infty\quad\text{for any}\quad j\neq k,

(2.9)

\displaystyle T_{y_{n}^{j}}^{-1}r_{n}^{J}\rightharpoonup 0\quad\text{weakly in }\quad H^{1}\quad\text{for each}\quad j\leq J

,

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}\left\lVert r_{n}^{J}\right\rVert_{L^{4}}=0.

(2.10)

Furthermore, we have

\mu\geqslant\mathbb{M}(u_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{M}(u_{j}),

(2.11)

H_{\infty}\geqslant\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{j}),

(2.12)

and (making use of (
2.10
))

G_{\infty}=\mathbb{G}(u_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{G}(u_{j}).

(2.13)

Note also that for any
f\in L^{2}+L^{\infty}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int f|\varphi_{n}|^{2}\,dx=\int f|u_{0}|^{2}\,dx.

(2.14)

Using (
2.14
), (
2.12
), and (
2.13
),
we obtain

\displaystyle 0={}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{n})

(2.15)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2H_{\infty}-3G_{\infty}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\tfrac{1}{2}(x\cdot\nabla V(x))|u_{0}(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geqslant{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j}),

and (recalling the definition of
\mathbb{I}_{V}
from Definition
1.2
)

E_{\infty}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{I}_{V}(\varphi_{n})=\mathbb{I}_{V}(u_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j}).

(2.16)

Step 2
.
In this step, we prove

\mathbb{K}_{V_{,}2}(u_{0})\leqslant 0.

(2.17)

In view of (
2.15
), it is a consequence of

\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j})\geqslant 0,\quad\forall j\geqslant 1.

(2.18)

Let us prove (
2.18
).
We fix
j_{0}\geqslant 1
and first observe that if
u_{j_{0}}=0
then
\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j_{0}})=0
, so we suppose instead that
u_{j_{0}}\neq 0
.

Using

\mathbb{I}_{V}(u_{0})\geqslant-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lVert(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)_{-}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\mathbb{M}(u_{0}),

(2.19)

we first observe that

\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j_{0}})<\tfrac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C\mu+\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lVert(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)_{-}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\mathbb{M}(u_{0}).

(2.20)

As
\mathbb{M}(u_{j_{0}})\leqslant\mu-\mathbb{M}(u_{0})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(u_{j_{0}})\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j_{0}})<{}

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mu-\mathbb{M}(u_{0})}{\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C\mu\mathbb{M}(u_{j_{0}})

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lVert(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)_{-}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}(\mu-\mathbb{M}(u_{0}))\mathbb{M}(u_{0}).

Now observe that if
\mu\lesssim_{Q,V}1
then the right-hand side is decreasing in
\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\in[0,\mu]
. Thus we obtain the bound

\mathbb{M}(u_{j_{0}})\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j_{0}})<\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C\mu\mathbb{M}(u_{j_{0}}).

(2.21)

Recalling that

\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)=\inf\{\mathbb{M}(\varphi)\mathbb{I}_{0}(\varphi)\ |\ \mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\varphi)\leqslant 0,\,\varphi\neq 0\},

(2.22)

we see from (
2.21
) that
\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j_{0}})>0
. Thus
\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j})\geqslant 0
for all
j\geqslant 1
, as desired.

Step 3
.
We next claim

\mathbb{G}(u_{0})\geq 1.

(2.23)

Notice that (
2.17
) and Proposition
2.3
imply one of the following two alternatives:

\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{0})\lesssim\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\leqslant\mu,

(2.24)

\mathbb{G}(u_{0})\gtrsim\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{0})\gtrsim\mathbb{M}(u_{0})^{-1}\geqslant\mu^{-1}.

(2.25)

To obtain the desired conclusion, it suffices to prove that (
2.25
) holds.

Now suppose towards a contradiction that (
2.24
) occurs. In this case, the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality implies

\mathbb{G}(u_{0})\lesssim\mathbb{M}(u_{0})^{2}

(2.26)

which yields

\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{0})\geqslant-C\mathbb{M}(u_{0})^{2}-\left\lVert(x\cdot\nabla V)_{+}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\geqslant-C^{\prime}\mathbb{M}(u_{0}).

(2.27)

We now claim that there exists unique
j_{1}\geqslant 1
such that

\mathbb{M}(u_{j_{1}})\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j_{1}})\geqslant(1-\mu)\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q).

(2.28)

The uniqueness of such a number is proved as follows: by Cauchy–Schwarz, embedding of
\ell^{p}
spaces, the mass decomposition, (
2.16
), and (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{M}(u_{j})\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{M}(u_{j})\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j})

\displaystyle\leqslant[\mu-\mathbb{M}(u_{0})][E_{\infty}-\mathbb{I}_{V}(u_{0})]

\displaystyle\leqslant[\mu-\mathbb{M}(u_{0})][\tfrac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C\mu+C_{V}\mathbb{M}(u_{0})]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C\mu^{2}+f_{\mu}(\mathbb{M}(u_{0})),

where

f_{\mu}(x):=\mu C_{V}x-\tfrac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)x+C\mu x-C_{V}x^{2}

is negative for sufficiently small
\mu
and
x\in[0,\mu]
. Hence, (
2.28
) does not hold for more than one
j_{1}
.

For the existence of such
j_{1}
, we suppose instead that

\mathbb{M}(u_{j})\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{j})<(1-\mu)\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)

(2.29)

for all
j\geqslant 1
. Then, by the sharp Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j})

\displaystyle\geqslant 2\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{j})-3\mathbb{G}(u_{j})^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\frac{\mathbb{G}(Q)}{\mathbb{M}(Q)^{1/2}\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q)^{3/2}}\mathbb{M}(u_{j})^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{j})^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle>2(1-(1-\mu)^{\frac{1}{3}})\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{j})>\tfrac{\mu}{3}\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{j})

for all
j\geqslant 1
, where we have used
\mathbb{I}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}
,
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q)=\tfrac{3}{2}\mathbb{G}(Q)
, and
(
2.29
). Thus, observing that

\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{j})\geqslant\tfrac{3}{2}\mathbb{G}(u_{j})\Leftrightarrow\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j})\geqslant 0,

we see from (
2.15
), (
2.18
), (
2.13
), (
2.26
),
(
2.11
), and the bound
G_{\infty}\gtrsim\mu^{-1}
that

\displaystyle-\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{0})\geqslant{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{j})>\tfrac{\mu}{3}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{j})\geqslant\tfrac{\mu}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{G}(u_{j})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mu}{2}(G_{\infty}-\mathbb{G}(u_{0}))>C(1-\mu^{3}),

which contradicts (
2.27
) if
\mu
is small. Thus we obtain
j_{1}
such that (
2.28
) holds, and by relabeling indices we may assume
j_{1}=1
. In particular,

\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{1})\geqslant\tfrac{1}{2\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q).

(2.30)

In view of (
2.20
) and (
2.28
),

\mu-\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\geqslant\mathbb{M}(u_{1})=\tfrac{\mathbb{M}(u_{1})\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{1})}{\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{1})}\geqslant\mu(1-\mu)\tfrac{\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)}{\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C_{1}\mu^{2}+C_{2}\mu\mathbb{M}(u_{0})},

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are constants depending only on
V
. Regarding this as a quadratic inequality with respect to
\mathbb{M}(u_{0})
and solving the inequality under the constraint
\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\leqslant\mu
, we obtain that in fact,

\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\lesssim\mu^{2}.

Using the assumption (
2.24
) and Gagliardo–Nirenberg, we also see that
\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{0})\lesssim\mu^{2}
and
\mathbb{G}(u_{0})\lesssim\mu^{4}
.
In particular, together with (
2.15
) and
(
2.18
),

0\leqslant\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{1})\leqslant-\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{0})\lesssim\mathbb{G}(u_{0})+\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\lesssim\mu^{2}.

(2.31)

Now, let

u_{1}^{\tau}=e^{\frac{3\tau}{2}}u_{1}(e^{\tau}x),

so that

\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{1}^{\tau})=2e^{2\tau}\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{1})-3e^{3\tau}\mathbb{G}(u_{1})=2e^{2\tau}\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{1})-6e^{2\tau}(e^{\tau}-1)\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{1}).

By (
2.30
) and (
2.31
), there exists
0<\tau_{0}\lesssim\mu^{3}
such that

\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{1}^{\tau_{0}})=0
. Then
\mathbb{M}(u_{1}^{\tau_{0}})=\mathbb{M}(u_{1})\leqslant\mu
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{1}^{\tau_{0}})=e^{3\tau_{0}}\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{1})\leqslant{}

\displaystyle(1+O(\mu^{3}))\left(\tfrac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-C_{1}^{\prime}\mu\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)-\tfrac{1}{2}C_{1}^{\prime}\mu

if
\mu
is small. Thus,
u_{1}^{\tau_{0}}
is a nonzero function satisfying

\mathbb{M}(u_{1}(\tau_{0}))\mathbb{I}_{0}(u_{1}(\tau_{0}))<\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(u_{1}(\tau_{0}))=0,

contradicting the characterization (
2.22
) of
Q
. Thus we conclude that (
2.24
) fails, so that (
2.25
) holds.

Step 4
.
Now, we see from (
2.11
),
(
2.17
), and (
2.23
) that
u_{0}
satisfies

\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\leqslant\mu,\quad\mathbb{K}_{V_{,}2}(u_{0})\leqslant 0,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{G}(u_{0})\geq 1,

One then deduces from the characterization (
2.7
) of
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
that

\mathbb{I}_{V}(u_{0})\geqslant\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu).

Furthermore, by (
2.16
),

\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)\leqslant\mathbb{I}_{V}(u_{0})\leqslant E_{\infty}\leqslant\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu).

Thus, we have
E_{\infty}=\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
and
u_{0}
is a minimzer to
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
. In fact, this also shows that if
E_{\infty}<\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
, then there is no sequence such that

\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{n})\to E_{\infty},\quad\mathbb{M}(\varphi_{n})\to\mu,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{n})\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty

and such that
\inf_{n}\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi_{n})\geqslant 1
. In particular, for small
\delta>0
, we have

\inf\{|\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi)|\ :\ \mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi)<\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)-\delta,\,\mathbb{M}(\varphi)\leqslant\mu,\,\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi)\geqslant 1\}\gtrsim_{\delta}1.

This will imply (
2.5
) once we establish
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)={\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
. In particular, we will complete the proof once we establish this identity and the existence of a minimizer to
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
.

We first show
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
. We select
\varphi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}
such that
\mathbb{M}(\varphi_{0})=\mu
and
\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{0})>\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)
. If there is no such function then
{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)=\infty
and the result follows. Otherwise,
\varphi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}
implies that
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{0})=0
.
Then, by Proposition
2.3
, we have either
\mathbb{H}_{0}\lesssim\mathbb{M}\lesssim\mu
or
\mu^{-1}\lesssim\mathbb{H}_{0}\lesssim\mathbb{G}(\varphi_{0})
. In the former case we see from Proposition
3.2
below that
2
2

2

This requires
\mu_{2}\leqslant\mu_{4}
.

\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{0})=\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)
, which is precluded.
Hence, we have the latter case. In particular,
\mathbb{G}(\varphi_{0})\geq 1
, so that

\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{0})\geqslant\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu).

Taking the infimum over such
\varphi_{0}
, we obtain
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)\leqslant{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
.

We next show
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)\geqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
. We let
\varphi_{1}
be the minimizer to
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
constructed above. Using a scaling argument, we may obtain that
\mathbb{M}(\varphi_{1})=\mu
and
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi_{1})=0
. Furthermore, arguing as in
[
19
]
, we may obtain that
\varphi_{1}\in\mathscr{S}
.
Noting that

\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{1})\gtrsim\mu^{-1}\geqslant\mu\sim\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu),

we therefore obtain

\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{1})\geqslant{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu).

Hence, the desired inequality follows from
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{1})
.
We may also observe that
\varphi_{1}
is in fact a minimizer to
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
.

Step 5
.
Let us next establish the bound (
2.4
).
The upper bound follows from Proposition
2.6
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)=\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(\mu)
.
Hence, we consider the lower bound.
It suffices to show
\mu\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)
tends to
\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)
as
\mu\to 0
.
To see this, we let
\varphi(\mu)
be the minimizer for
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)
and set
Q_{\mu}(x):=\mu\varphi(\mu)(\mu x)
.
A computation shows
\mathbb{M}(Q_{\mu})=1
and, thanks to (
2.25
),
\mathbb{G}(Q_{\mu})\gtrsim 1
. Further,

\displaystyle\mu\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu)=\mu\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi(\mu))=\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q_{\mu})-\mathbb{G}(Q_{\mu})+\tfrac{\mu^{2}}{2}\int V(\mu x)|Q_{\mu}|^{2}dx

and

0=\mu\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(\varphi(\mu))=2\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q_{\mu})-3\mathbb{G}(Q_{\mu})+\tfrac{\mu^{2}}{4}\int(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)(\mu x)|Q_{\mu}|^{2}dx

follow. Combining these two identities and using the upper bound in (
2.4
),
\mathbb{M}(Q_{\mu})=1
, and the assumption
V,x\cdot\nabla V\in L^{\infty}
, one has

\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q_{\mu})<3\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)+C\mu^{2},

where the constant
C
depends only on
V
. Hence,
\{Q_{\mu}\}_{0<\mu\leqslant\mu_{2}}
is a bounded sequence in
H^{1}
.
Further,

1\lesssim\varlimsup_{\mu\to 0}\mathbb{G}(Q_{\mu})=\varlimsup_{\mu\to 0}\tfrac{2}{3}\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q_{\mu})\leqslant 2\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)

Now, pick a sequence
\mu_{n}\to 0
. Subtracting a subsequence if necessary,
we suppose that
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q_{\mu_{n}})
and
\mathbb{G}(Q_{\mu_{n}})
converge.
We apply the profile decomposition to
\{Q_{\mu_{n}}\}_{n}
with remainder vanishing in
L^{4}
, as in Step 1. This now takes the form

Q_{\mu_{n}}=\sum_{j=0}^{J}T_{y_{n}^{j}}\tilde{Q}_{j}+\rho_{n}^{J}

up to a subsequence. Note that we have the bounds

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{M}(\tilde{Q}_{j})\leqslant 1,\quad\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{G}(\tilde{Q}_{j})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{G}(Q_{\mu_{n}})\leqslant 2\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q).

(2.32)

If
\tilde{Q}_{j}=0
for all
j\geqslant 0
then we have
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{G}(\tilde{Q}_{j})=0
, a contradiction.
Hence,
\tilde{Q}_{j}\neq 0
for some
j\geqslant 0
.
Then, since

\sum_{j=0}\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\tilde{Q}_{j})\leqslant 2\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{H}_{0}(Q_{\mu_{n}})-3\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{G}(Q_{\mu_{n}})=0,

there exists
j_{2}\geqslant 0
such that
\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}}\neq 0
and

\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})\leqslant 0
.
Then, recalling
\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)=\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)=\mathbb{G}(Q)/2
, we see from (
2.22
) and (
2.32
) that

\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)=\mathbb{I}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)\leqslant\mathbb{I}_{0}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})\mathbb{M}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})\mathbb{M}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q).

In particular,
\mathbb{G}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})\mathbb{M}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})=2\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)
holds.
This also implies that
\mathbb{M}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})=1
in view of (
2.32
).
Hence
\tilde{Q}_{j}=0
for all
j\neq j_{2}
and further
Q_{\mu_{n}}
converges
strongly to
\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}}
in
H^{1}
.
Thus,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu_{n})=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\tilde{Q}_{j_{2}})=\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q).

Since
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n}
is arbitrary, we obtain the desired convergence.

Step 6
.
Finally, let us prove continuity of
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
.
Let
\varphi(\mu)
denote the minimizer for
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
. We define

{\varphi}^{\tau}(x)=e^{6\tau/5}(\varphi(\mu))(e^{\tau}x).

It follows that

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\mathbb{M}({\varphi}^{\tau})|_{\tau=0}=-\tfrac{3}{5}\mu,

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\mathbb{E}_{V}({\varphi}^{\tau})|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{7}{5}\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi(\mu))-\tfrac{9}{5}\mathbb{G}(\varphi(\mu))+O(1)=\tfrac{1}{5}\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi(\mu))+O(1),

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{d\tau}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}({\varphi}^{\tau})|_{\tau=0}=\tfrac{14}{5}\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi(\mu))-\tfrac{27}{5}\mathbb{G}(\varphi(\mu))+O(1)=-\tfrac{4}{5}\mathbb{H}_{0}(\varphi(\mu))+O(1).

This shows that
\mathbb{E}_{V}({\varphi}^{\tau})\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)+O(\tau\mu^{-1})
for small
\tau>0
. Thus, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta_{0}=O(\mu^{2}\varepsilon)
so that if
\mu^{\prime}\in(\mu-\delta_{0},\mu)
then

\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu^{\prime})\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)+\varepsilon.

This implies left continuity of
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
. The right continuity is shown similarly. ∎

We turn now to the estimate on
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
that was needed in the proof above.

Proposition 2.6
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\mu_{2}^{\prime}>0
such that

\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)\leqslant\tfrac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)+(V(0)+\varepsilon)\mu\quad\text{for all}\quad 0<\mu<\mu_{2}^{\prime},

where
\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)
is defined in (
2.6
).

Proof.

Recalling (
2.7
) and using the rescaling
\psi(x)=\mu\varphi(\mu x)
, we may write

\mu\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)=\inf_{\psi\in A_{\mu}}\biggl[\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\psi)+\tfrac{1}{4}\int\mu^{2}(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)(\mu x)|\psi(x)|^{2}dx\biggr],

(2.33)

where

A_{\mu}=\bigl\{\psi\in H^{1}\ |\ \mathbb{M}(\psi)\leqslant 1,\,\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\psi)\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}\int\mu^{2}(x\cdot\nabla V)(\mu x)|\psi(x)|^{2}dx,\,\mathbb{G}(\psi)\geqslant\mu\bigr\}.

Now set
\psi_{1}^{a}=e^{a}Q(e^{a}x)
for
a\in\mathbb{R}
, where
Q
is the NLS ground state as above. We recall that

\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(Q)=0,\quad\mathbb{G}(Q)=2\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q),

and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(\psi_{1}^{a})=e^{-a}\mathbb{M}(Q),

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\psi_{1}^{a})=\tfrac{1}{2}e^{a}\mathbb{G}(Q)=e^{a}\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q),

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\psi_{1}^{a})=e^{a}\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(Q)=0.

We then let
\tilde{\psi}_{1}:=\psi_{1}^{a_{0}}
with
a_{0}=\log\mathbb{M}(Q)
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})=1,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q),

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})=2\mathbb{H}_{0}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})-3\mathbb{G}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})=0.

Finally, set
\psi_{2}^{\tau}=e^{3\tau/2}\tilde{\psi}_{1}(e^{\tau}x)
for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})=\mathbb{M}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})=1,

(2.34)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})=\tfrac{1}{2}e^{3\tau}\mathbb{G}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})=e^{3\tau}\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q),

(2.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})=2e^{2\tau}\mathbb{H}_{0}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})-3e^{3\tau}\mathbb{G}(\tilde{\psi}_{1})=6e^{2\tau}(1-e^{\tau})\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q).

(2.36)

We will choose
\tau
so that
\psi_{2}^{\tau}\in A_{\mu}
. To this end, we first observe that (
2.34
)– (
2.35
) yield the mass constrant, while the constraint
\mathbb{G}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})\geqslant\mu
is also satisfied provided
|\tau|\lesssim_{Q}1
and
\mu\lesssim_{Q}1
. To obtain the constraint on
\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})
, we claim that

\tfrac{1}{2}\int\mu^{2}(x\cdot\nabla V)(\mu x)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}dx=o(\mu^{2})

(2.37)

as
\mu\to 0
, locally uniformly in
\tau
. Indeed, since
x\cdot\nabla V
is bounded and continuous at the origin, the dominated convergence theorem implies

\lim_{\mu\to 0}\tfrac{1}{2}\int(x\cdot\nabla V)(\mu x)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}dx=\tfrac{1}{2}\int(x\cdot\nabla V)(0)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}dx=0

locally uniformly in
\tau
, which yields (
2.37
). Thus, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\tilde{\mu}>0
such that

\left\lvert\int(x\cdot\nabla V)(\mu x)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}\right\rvert\leqslant 12\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)\quad\text{for}\quad\mu\in(0,\tilde{\mu}).

The choice
\tau=\varepsilon^{2}\mu^{2}
in (
2.36
) then yields

\mathbb{K}_{0,2}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})\leqslant-6\varepsilon^{2}\mu^{2}\mathbb{E}^{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)\leqslant-\mu^{2}\left\lvert\tfrac{1}{2}\int(x\cdot\nabla V)(\mu x)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}dx\right\rvert\leqslant 0

for
\mu\in(0,\tilde{\mu})
, which implies
\psi_{2}^{\tau}\in A_{\mu}
.

Next, arguing as in the proof of (
2.37
), we observe that

\tfrac{1}{4}\int(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)(\mu x)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}\,dx\to V(0)\mathbb{M}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})=V(0)<0

as
\mu\to 0
by virtue of (
2.34
).
We remark that this covergence also holds locally uniformly in
\tau
.
Thus, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}>0
such that

\left\lvert\tfrac{1}{4}\int(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)(\mu x)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}dx-V(0)\right\rvert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

(2.38)

if
\mu\in(0,\tilde{\mu}^{\prime})
and
|\tau|\leqslant 1
.
We now take
\varepsilon>0
so small that

\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{6(e-1)\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)}

is satisfied
and fix the corresponding
\tilde{\mu}
and
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}
.
Then, there exists
\mu_{2}^{\prime}\in(0,\min(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\mu}^{\prime})]
such that the choice
\tau=\varepsilon^{2}\mu^{2}
yields
\tau\leqslant 1
and

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})={}

\displaystyle e^{3\tau}\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)

(2.39)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle(1+3(e-1)\tau)\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)+(3(e-1)\varepsilon\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q))\varepsilon\mu^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\mu^{2}

for
\mu\in(0,\mu_{2}^{\prime})
, where we have used (
2.35
).
Letting
\tau=\varepsilon^{2}\mu^{2}
and
plugging
\psi_{2}^{\tau}\in A_{\mu}
, (
2.38
), and (
2.39
)
to the formula (
2.33
), one concludes that

\displaystyle\mu\tilde{\mathscr{E}}_{1}(\mu)

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{G}(\psi_{2}^{\tau})+\tfrac{1}{4}\int\mu^{2}(x\cdot\nabla V+2V)(\mu x)|\psi_{2}^{\tau}(x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\mu^{2}+\left(V(0)+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)\mu^{2}

\displaystyle{}=\mathbb{E}_{0}(Q)\mathbb{M}(Q)+({V(0)}+\varepsilon)\mu^{2}

for
\mu\in(0,\mu_{2}^{\prime})
, which yields the desired result. ∎

We turn our attention now to the defocusing case,
\sigma=-1
, in which case we expect that the excited solitons are not present (see e.g.
[
19
, Proposition 2.6]
for the radial case). In the present setting, we can immediately see that there are no solutions to (
2.1
) in the case
\omega\geqslant-e_{0}
. Indeed, taking the
L^{2}
inner product of (
2.1
) with
\bar{\varphi}
, we have

(H\varphi,\varphi)+\omega\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=-\|\varphi\|_{L^{4}}^{4}.

Since,
H\geqslant e_{0}
, we obtain

(e_{0}+\omega)\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant-\|\varphi\|_{L^{4}}^{4},

which implies the claim. More generally, we conjecture the following:

Conjecture 2.1

(Solitons in the defocusing case)
.

Let
\sigma=-1
. The equation (
2.1
) has a unique positive solution
\varphi_{\omega}
for
\omega\in(0,-e_{0})
. The set
\mathscr{S}
is characterized as

\mathscr{S}=\{e^{i\theta}\varphi_{\omega}\ |\ \omega\in(0,-e_{0}),\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The map
(0,-e_{0})\ni\omega\mapsto\mathbb{M}(\varphi_{\omega})\in(0,\infty)
is monotone decreasing
C^{1}
function. Denoting the inverse function by
\omega_{0}(\mu)
, we have
\omega_{0}^{\prime}(\mu)<0
and

\mathscr{E}_{0}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{V}(\varphi_{\omega_{0}(\mu)})\in(e_{0}\mu,0)\quad\text{for all}\quad\mu>0.

On the other hand,
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)=\infty
for all
\mu>0
.

In fact, most of Conjecture
2.1
may be found in
[
10
]
; the only claim that has not yet been established is that
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)=\infty
for all
\mu>0
in the non-radial case.

3.
Global dynamics

In this section, we prove the main result (Theorem
1.1
), taking for granted one key proposition (Proposition
3.7
). The remaining sections will then be devoted to establishing Proposition
3.7
.

We begin with the following result concerning global existence for (
1.1
).

Proposition 3.1

(Global existence)
.

Let
\mu_{2}
be as in Proposition
2.4
. Suppose
u
is a solution to (
1.1
) such that

\mathbb{M}(u)=\mu\leqslant\mu_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{V}(u)<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mathbb{M}(u)).

If
\sigma=1
and

\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}>1\quad\text{and}\quad K_{V,2}(u(t))<0

fails at some time
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then it fails for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and the solution
u
is global. Furthermore, the solution satisfies one of the following:

•

We have
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\mu
and scattering to
\mathscr{S}_{0}
holds in both time directions.

•

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mu\lesssim\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\mathbb{E}_{V}(u)+\mu\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u(t))\geqslant\kappa

for some
\kappa>0
, with the implicit constants is independent of
\kappa
.

If
\sigma=-1
, then the solution is global and one of the two alternatives above holds.

Proof.

If
\sigma=-1
, then conservation of energy yields an
a priori
bound on the
H^{1}
norm of
u
(note that the contribution of the potential is controlled by the small mass assumption). In particular, the solution is global. Furthermore, the dichotomy follows from the coercivity of
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}
under the small mass assumption (cf. Proposition
2.3
).

Suppose instead
\sigma=1
. Using (
2.5
) and the trichotomy of Proposition
2.3
, we find that the validity of

\mathbb{H}_{0}(u(t))>1\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u(t))<0

is preserved in time. Thus, the failure of this property is preserved as well.

Now, if
\mathbb{H}_{0}(u(t))\leqslant 1
and
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u(t))<0
then we see from the trichotomy of Proposition
2.3
that

\mathbb{H}_{0}(u(t))\lesssim\mu
, which implies that the first alternative holds (see
[
10
]
for the scattering result).

If instead
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u(t))\geqslant 0
then Sobolev embedding and Young’s inequality yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}(u(t))

\displaystyle=3\mathbb{E}_{V}(u)-\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u(t))-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(x\cdot\nabla V+3V)|u(t,x)|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\mathbb{E}_{V}(u)+C\mathbb{M}(u)+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{H}_{0}(u(t))

for some
C>0
. In particular, we have
\mathbb{H}_{0}(u(t))\leqslant 6\mathbb{E}_{V}(u)+2C\mathbb{M}(u)
, which guarantees global existence via energy-subcriticality. The remaining bounds are again a consequence of Proposition
2.3
. ∎

For global solutions to (
1.1
) with small mass, we will introduce a decomposition into a ground state part and a ‘radiation’ part. The equation (
1.1
) then reduces to a coupled ODE-PDE system for the ground state parameter and the radiation (see (
3.8
) below).

We begin by recalling the ground state map
\Phi
introduced in Lemma
2.1
. We introduce
D_{j}
to denote the derivative with respect to the real and imaginary part of
z=z^{1}+iz^{2}
. That is, we view
\Phi(z)=\Phi(z^{1},z^{2})
and denote

\displaystyle D_{1}\Phi(z)

\displaystyle{}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}}\Phi(z^{1},z^{2}),

\displaystyle D_{2}\Phi(z)

\displaystyle{}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{2}}\Phi(z^{1},z^{2}).

For
z\in\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<2z_{1}\}\backslash\{0\}
, we define

P_{c}[z]H^{1}:=\{\varphi\in H^{1}\ |\ \operatorname{Re}(i\varphi,D_{j}\Phi[z])=0\text{ for }j=1,2\}

and for
z=0
:

P_{c}[0]H^{1}:=\{\varphi\in H^{1}\ |\ (\varphi,\phi_{0})=0\}.

To simplify notation, we write
P_{c}H^{1}=P_{c}[0]H^{1}
.

We define
D\Phi(z):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
via

(D\Phi(z))w:=\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}\Phi(z)&D_{2}\Phi(z)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}w^{1}\\
w^{2}\end{pmatrix},

which we may also view as a map from
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
via the identification

z^{1}+iz^{2}\leftrightarrow\begin{pmatrix}z^{1}\\
z^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Observe that
D\Phi
is
\mathbb{R}
-linear but not
\mathbb{C}
-linear. Indeed,

(D\Phi(z))(iw)=\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}\Phi(z)&D_{2}\Phi(z)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\operatorname{Im}w\\
\operatorname{Re}w\end{pmatrix}

and

i(D\Phi(z))w=i\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}\Phi(z)&D_{2}\Phi(z)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Re}w\\
\operatorname{Im}w\end{pmatrix}

are not identical in general.

We note also that

\tfrac{d}{dt}\Phi(z(t))=\begin{pmatrix}D_{1}\Phi(z)&D_{2}\Phi(z)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\dot{z}^{1}\\
\dot{z}^{2}\end{pmatrix}=(D\Phi(z))\dot{z},

(3.1)

and that by the gauge invariance
\Phi(re^{i\theta})=e^{i\theta}\Phi(r)
, one has

(D\Phi(z))(iz)=i\Phi(z).

(3.2)

In what follows, we use the notation

\mathcal{B}_{\mu}:=\{\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\ |\ \mathbb{M}(\varphi)<\mu\}.

The following result (appearing as
[
10
, Lemma 2.3]
) allows us to isolate the small soliton component for
H^{1}
functions with small mass.

Proposition 3.2

(Decomposition around ground states)
.

There exist
\mu_{4}\in(0,\mu_{0}]
and a unique mapping

\mathcal{B}_{\mu_{4}}\ni\varphi\mapsto(z,\eta)\in\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<2z_{1}\}\times\mathcal{B}_{\mu_{4}}

such that

\displaystyle\varphi=\Phi[z]+\eta,\qquad\eta\in\mathcal{B}_{\mu_{4}}\cap P_{c}[z]H^{1}.

Moreover,

\mathbb{M}(\varphi)=\mathbb{M}(\Phi[z])+\mathbb{M}(\eta).

The map
\varphi\mapsto(z,\eta)
is smooth and injective from
\mathcal{B}_{\mu_{4}}
to
\mathbb{C}\times H^{1}
.

We introduce the
\mathbb{C}
-linear, bounded projection operator
P_{c}:H^{1}\to P_{c}H^{1}
via

P_{c}\phi:=\phi-(\phi,\phi_{0})\phi_{0}.

We then have the following lemma relating
P_{c}
and
P_{c}[z]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists
\mu_{5}\in(0,\mu_{4}]
and
z_{5}\in(0,z_{1}]
such that if
|z|\leqslant 2z_{5}
, then
P_{c}|_{P_{c}[z]H^{1}}
is invertible.
The inverse
R[z]
is of the form

R[z]\phi=\phi-\rho(z,\phi)\phi_{0},\quad\phi\in P_{c}H^{1},

where
\rho\in\mathbb{C}
is linear with respect to
\phi
and satisfies

|\rho(z,\phi)|\lesssim\sum_{j=1,2}|\operatorname{Re}(i\phi,D_{j}\Phi[z])|=\sum_{j=1,2}|\operatorname{Re}(i\phi,D_{j}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}))|.

(3.3)

Remark 3.4
.

The operator
R[z]
is a compact perturbation of the identity operator
on any space between
H^{2}\cap H^{1}_{1}
and
H^{-2}+L^{\infty}
.

Proof.

The map
\rho
will be defined to impose that
R[z]\phi\in P_{c}[z]H^{1}
, which requires the orthogonality conditions

0=\operatorname{Re}(iR[z]\phi,D_{j}\Phi[z])=\operatorname{Re}i(\phi,D_{j}\Phi[z])-\operatorname{Re}i\rho(z,\phi)(\phi_{0},D_{j}\Phi[z])

for
j=1,2.
Using
D_{1}(z\phi_{0})=\phi_{0}
and
D_{2}(z\phi_{0})=i\phi_{0}
, we see that for
\phi\in P_{c}
these conditions are equivalent to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}i\rho(z,\phi)(\phi_{0},D_{1}\Phi[z])=\operatorname{Re}i\rho(z,\phi)(1+(\phi_{0},D_{1}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}))),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}i\rho(z,\phi)(\phi_{0},D_{2}\Phi[z])=\operatorname{Re}i\rho(z,\phi)(i+(\phi_{0},D_{2}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}))).

Thus,
\rho
may be defined via

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{0},D_{1}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}))&-1-\operatorname{Re}(\phi_{0},D_{1}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}))\\
-1-\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{0},D_{2}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}))&-\operatorname{Re}(\phi_{0},D_{2}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}))\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\operatorname{Re}\rho(z,\phi)\\
\operatorname{Im}\rho(z,\phi)\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\operatorname{Re}i(\phi,D_{1}\Phi[z])\\
\operatorname{Re}i(\phi,D_{2}\Phi[z])\end{bmatrix},

provided the coefficient matrix above is invertible. In particular, invertibility from the fact that
\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0}=O(z^{2})
in
H^{1}
if
z_{5}
is sufficiently small. Furthermore, we obtain the estimate

|\rho(z,\phi)|\leqslant 2\sum_{j=1,2}|\operatorname{Re}(i\phi,D_{j}\Phi[z])|.

As

\operatorname{Re}(i\phi,D_{j}\Phi[z])=\operatorname{Re}(i\phi,D_{j}(\Phi[z]-z\phi_{0})),

this completes the proof.∎

We now consider a small mass solution
u(t)
to (
1.1
), which we write as

u(t)=\Phi[z(t)]+\eta(t)

according to Proposition
3.2
. We can then derive the evolution equations for
\eta(t)
and
z(t)
as follows. We consider only the focusing case
\sigma=+1
, noting that trivial modifications handle the defocusing case.

Using (
1.1
), (
2.2
), (
3.1
), and (
3.2
), the equation for
\eta
may be written

i{\partial}_{t}\eta+H\eta=\tilde{B}[z]\eta+N(z,\eta)-i(D\Phi(z))(\dot{z}+i\Omega z),

(3.4)

where
\tilde{B}[z]
is the
\mathbb{R}
-linear operator

\tilde{B}[z]f:=2|\Phi[z]|^{2}f+\Phi[z]^{2}\bar{f}

and
N(z,\eta)
collects the quadratic and cubic nonlinear terms as follows:

N(z,\eta):=2|\eta|^{2}\Phi[z]+\eta^{2}\overline{\Phi[z]}+|\eta|^{2}\eta.

Writing
\xi:=P_{c}\eta
, we have
P_{c}{\partial}_{t}\eta={\partial}_{t}\xi
, and hence
{\partial}_{t}\eta=R[z]{\partial}_{t}\xi.
Furthermore,

H\xi=HP_{c}\eta=H\eta-H\phi_{0}(\eta,\phi_{0})=H\eta-\phi_{0}(H\eta,\phi_{0})=P_{c}H\eta,

so that
H\eta=R[z]H\xi.
Thus we obtain the following equation for
\xi
:

i{\partial}_{t}\xi+H\xi=B[z]\xi+P_{c}N(z,R[z]\xi)-iP_{c}(D\Phi(z))(\dot{z}+i\Omega z),

(3.5)

where
B[z]\xi:=P_{c}\tilde{B}[z]R[z]\xi.

We turn to the equation for
z
. We first differentiate the orthogonality conditions

\operatorname{Re}(i\eta,D_{j}\Phi(z))=0

with respect to
t
. Then, using (
3.4
) and the identities

HD_{j}\Phi+D_{j}(\Omega\Phi)=D_{j}(|\Phi|^{2}\Phi),

we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}(i\eta,(D(D_{j}\Phi))\dot{z})

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Re}(\eta,\Omega D_{j}\Phi)-\operatorname{Re}(i(D\Phi)(\dot{z}+i\Omega z),D_{j}\Phi)

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Re}(N(z,\eta),D_{j}\Phi).

Furthermore, since
\eta\in P_{c}[z]H^{1}
,

\operatorname{Re}(\eta,\Omega D_{j}\Phi)=\operatorname{Re}(i\eta,\Omega D(D_{j}\Phi)iz).

Thus,

\operatorname{Re}(i(D\Phi)(\dot{z}+i\Omega z),D_{j}\Phi)-\operatorname{Re}(i\eta,(D(D_{j}\Phi))(\dot{z}+i\Omega z))=\operatorname{Re}(N(z,\eta),D_{j}\Phi),

we may rewrite in the form

\begin{pmatrix}M_{11}&M_{12}\\
M_{13}&M_{14}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Re}(\dot{z}+i\Omega z)\\
\operatorname{Im}(\dot{z}+i\Omega z)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Re}(N(z,\eta),D_{1}\Phi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(N(z,\eta),D_{2}\Phi)\end{pmatrix}

with

M_{jk}=M_{jk}(z,\eta)=\operatorname{Re}(iD_{k}\Phi,D_{j}\Phi)-\operatorname{Re}(i\eta,D_{j}D_{k}\Phi).

Thus, we obtain

\dot{z}+i\Omega z=\underline{N}(z,\eta):=\begin{pmatrix}1&i\end{pmatrix}M^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Re}(N(z,\eta),D_{1}\Phi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(N(z,\eta),D_{2}\Phi)\end{pmatrix}.

(3.6)

Introducing the notation

\tilde{N}(z,\xi):=P_{c}(N(z,R[z]\xi)-i(D\Phi(z))\underline{N}(z,R[z]\xi))

(3.7)

and combining (
3.6
) and (
3.5
), we obtain the following PDE-ODE system for the pair
(\xi,z)
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &i{\partial}_{t}\xi+H\xi=B[z]\xi+\tilde{N}(z,\xi),\\
&\dot{z}+i\Omega z=\underline{N}(z,R[z]\xi).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.8)

We now turn to the scattering result of Theorem
1.1
, first in the focusing case. For
\mu>0
and
A\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\mathrm{GS}(\mu,A)
to be the set of global,
H^{1}
-bounded solutions
u
to (
1.1
) with
\mathbb{M}(u)\leqslant\mu
and
\mathbb{E}_{V}(u)\leqslant A.
We then define

\displaystyle\mathrm{ST}(\mu,A):={}

\displaystyle\sup\{\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{8}L^{4}(\mathbb{R})}\ |\ \Phi[z]+R[z]\xi\in\mathrm{GS}(\mu,A)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}:={}

\displaystyle\{(\mu,A)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ \mathrm{ST}(\mu,A)<\infty\}.

Observing that
\mathrm{ST}(\mu,A)<\infty
yields scattering to
\mathscr{S}_{0}
(see e.g.
[
19
, Lemma 6.2]
), the scattering portion of Theorem
1.1
in the focusing case may be rewritten as follows:

Theorem 3.5

(Scattering to the ground states)
.

For
\mu>0
sufficiently small,

(\mu,A)\in\mathcal{X}\Longleftrightarrow A<\mathcal{E}_{1}(\mu).

The
\implies
direction of Theorem
3.5
follows from the existence of an excited state, i.e. Proposition
2.4
. Thus it remains to prove the
\Longleftarrow
direction.

By the small data theory, we have that for small enough
\mu>0
, we have that
(\mu,A)\in\mathcal{X}
for all
A
sufficiently small. To prove the theorem, we suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
(\mu_{0},A_{0})
and
\delta_{0}>0
such that

\mu_{0}\ll 1,\quad A_{0}=\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu_{0})-\delta_{0}<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu_{0}),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{ST}(\mu_{0},A_{0})=\infty.

We then define

\displaystyle E_{*}

\displaystyle:=\sup\{A<A_{0}\ |\ (\mu_{0},A)\in\mathcal{X}\}\leqslant A_{0},

(3.9)

\displaystyle M_{*}

\displaystyle:=\sup\{\mu<\mu_{0}\ |\ (\mu,E_{*})\in\mathcal{X}\}\leqslant\mu_{0}.

As
\mu\mapsto\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
is decreasing, we have

E_{*}\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu_{0})-\delta_{0}\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(M_{*})-\delta_{0}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{M_{*}}\mathbb{M}(Q)\mathbb{E}(Q)-CM_{*}-\delta_{0}.

(3.10)

Next, we observe that

\bigl((0,M_{*}]\times(0,E_{*}]\bigr)\setminus(M_{*},E_{*})\subset\mathcal{X}.

(3.11)

Indeed, by definition of
E_{*}
, we have that
0<E<E_{*}\implies(\mu_{0},E)\in\mathcal{X}
. Furthermore, by definition of
\mathrm{ST}(\mu,A)
, if
(\mu_{0},E)\in\mathcal{X}
then
(\mu,E)\in\mathcal{X}
for all
0<\mu\leqslant\mu_{0}
. Thus
(0,\mu_{0}]\times(0,E_{*})\subset\mathcal{X}.
As the definition of
M_{*}
implies
(0,M_{*})\times\{E_{*}\}\subset\mathcal{X}
, we obtain (
3.11
).

We also claim that

(M_{*},\infty)\times(E_{*},\infty)\subset\mathcal{X}^{c}.

(3.12)

To see this, we argue as follows:

First, if
(M_{*},E_{*})=(\mu_{0},E_{0})\not\in\mathcal{X}
, then (
3.12
) follows immediately.

Second, if
M_{*}<\mu_{0}
then the definition of
M_{*}
shows that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
M^{*}\in(M_{*},M_{*}+\varepsilon)
such that
(M^{*},E_{*})\not\in\mathcal{X}
, which implies that
[M_{*}+\varepsilon,\infty)\times[E_{*},\infty)\subset\mathcal{X}^{c}
. As
\varepsilon>0
may be taken arbitrarily, (
3.12
) follows.

Finally, if
M_{*}=\mu_{0}
and
E_{*}<E_{0}
then the definition of
E_{*}
shows that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
E^{*}\in(E_{*},E_{*}+\varepsilon)
such that

(\mu_{0},E^{*})\not\in\mathcal{X}
, which implies that
[\mu_{0},\infty)\times[E_{*}+\varepsilon,\infty)\subset\mathcal{X}^{c}
. Again, since
\varepsilon>0
may be taken arbitrarily, (
3.12
) follows.

Now, using the facts that
M_{*}\leqslant\mu_{0}
and
E_{*}\leq A_{0}<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu_{0})
, we see from the continuity of
\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)
that

(M_{*}+\varepsilon_{0},E_{*}+\varepsilon_{0})\in\{(\mu,A)\ |\ A<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu)\}

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{0}
. Thus there exists a sequence of global solutions
u_{n}=\Phi[z_{n}]+R[z_{n}]\xi_{n}
obeying

\mathbb{M}(u_{n})=M_{*}+o_{n}(1),\quad\mathbb{E}_{V}(u_{n})=E_{*}+o_{n}(1),

(3.13)

and

\min\bigl\{\left\lVert\xi_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{8}_{t}L^{4}_{x}(\mathbb{R}_{+})},\left\lVert\xi_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{8}_{t}L^{4}_{x}(\mathbb{R}_{-})}\bigr\}\to\infty\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty,

(3.14)

with

\mathbb{E}_{V}(u_{n})\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mathbb{M}(u_{n}))-\tfrac{\delta_{0}}{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad n.

Furthermore, Proposition
3.1
implies

\inf_{n\geq 1}\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{n}(t))\geqslant\kappa(\delta_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{n}\left\lVert u_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},H^{1})}^{2}\lesssim E_{*}+M_{*}.

(3.15)

Continuing from above, we seek to obtain a contradiction under the assumption

M_{*}\leqslant\mu_{0},\quad E_{*}\leqslant\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mu_{0})-\delta_{0}.

(3.16)

The key ingredient is the following:

Proposition 3.6

(Existence of a compact non-scattering solution)
.

Suppose that (
3.16
) holds. Then there exists a global solution

u_{*}(t)=\Phi[z_{*}(t)]+R[z_{*}(t)]\xi_{*}(t)\in C(\mathbb{R},H^{1})

to (
1.1
) such that

\mathbb{M}(u_{*})=M_{*},\quad\mathbb{E}_{V}(u_{*})=E_{*},\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert u_{*}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim E_{*}+M_{*}.

Furthermore,
u_{*}
does not scatter to
\mathscr{S}_{0}
in either time direction, i.e.

\left\lVert\xi_{*}\right\rVert_{L^{8}_{t}L^{4}_{x}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}=\left\lVert\xi_{*}\right\rVert_{L^{8}_{t}L^{4}_{x}(\mathbb{R}_{-})}=\infty.

Finally,

\{u_{*}(t)\ |\ t\in\mathbb{R}\}\quad\text{is pre-compact in}\quad H^{1}.

(3.17)

Proposition
3.6
is in turn a consequence of the following.

Proposition 3.7

(Key convergence result)
.

Suppose that (
3.16
) holds. Let

u_{n}(t)=\Phi[z_{n}(t)]+R[z_{n}(t)]\xi_{n}(t)

be a sequence of global solutions to (
1.1
) such that (
3.13
), (
3.14
), and (
3.15
) hold. Then, there exist a subsequence in
n
and a function
u_{0,\infty}\in H^{1}
such that

u_{n}(0)\to u_{0,\infty}\quad\text{in }H^{1}\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty

along this subsequence. The solution

u_{\infty}(t)=\Phi[z_{\infty}(t)]+R[z_{\infty}(t)]\xi_{\infty}(t)\in C(\mathbb{R},H^{1})

to (
1.1
) with data
u_{\infty}(0)=u_{0,\infty}
exists globally in time and satisfies the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(u_{\infty})=M_{*},\quad\mathbb{E}_{V}(u_{\infty})=E_{*},\quad\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{\infty}(t))\geqslant\kappa(\delta_{0}),

and
\left\lVert u_{\infty}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\lesssim E_{*}+M_{*}.
Moreover,
u_{\infty}(t)
does not scatter to
\mathscr{S}_{0}
in either time direction, i.e.
\left\lVert\xi_{\infty}\right\rVert_{L^{8}_{t}L^{4}_{x}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}=\left\lVert\xi_{\infty}\right\rVert_{L^{8}_{t}L^{4}_{x}(\mathbb{R}_{-})}=\infty.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
, assuming Proposition
3.7
.

We have already constructed solutions obeying the assumptions of Proposition
3.7
. Thus we obtain the global solution
u_{\infty}
, and it remains only to verify the pre-compactness. In fact, given
\{t_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
,
u_{n}(t):=u_{\infty}(t+t_{n})
is a sequence of solutions obeying the assumptions of Proposition
3.7
. Thus we we can extract a subsequence such that
u_{n}(0)=u_{*}(t_{n})
converges strongly in
H^{1}
. ∎

The proof of Proposition
3.7
(given in Section
6
) in the non-radial setting is the key new ingredient in this paper. For now, we take Proposition
3.7
(and hence Proposition
3.6
) for granted and complete the proof of the scattering part of Theorem
1.1
(cf. Theorem
3.5
above). As in
[
19
]
, the proof relies on a localized virial argument, which shows that a solution as in Proposition
3.6
cannot exist. In
[
19
]
, an essential role played by the radial assumption is to guarantee that the critical element is compact in
H^{1}
without the need for a moving spatial center. Proposition
3.6
asserts that the same is true in the non-radial setting; however, proving this is a non-trivial task, comprising the majority of the work in this paper.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Continuing from above, our task is to reach a contradiction under the assumption (
3.16
). To this end, we apply Proposition
3.6
to obtain the solution
u_{*}
, which satisfies

\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{*}(t))\geqslant\kappa_{*}>0.

Let
\zeta(x)
be a non-negative radial smooth function such that

\zeta(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &|x|&&|x|\leqslant 1,\\
&\tfrac{3}{2}&&|x|\geqslant 2.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.18)

and
\zeta_{R}(x)=\zeta(x/R)
for
R>0
. We define

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\zeta_{0,R}|\nabla f|^{2}+\zeta_{1,R}|\tfrac{x}{|x|}\cdot\nabla f|^{2}+\left(\tfrac{1}{R}\right)^{2}\zeta_{2,R}|f|^{2}+\zeta_{3,R}|f|^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad-\tfrac{1}{2}\zeta_{0,R}(x\cdot\nabla V)|f|^{2}\,dx,

(3.19)

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{0}

\displaystyle{}=1-|x|^{-1}\zeta,

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}

\displaystyle{}=|x|^{-1}\zeta-{\partial}_{r}\zeta,

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}

\displaystyle{}=\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta[\partial_{r}+\tfrac{2}{|x|}],

\displaystyle\zeta_{3}

\displaystyle{}=-\tfrac{1}{4}[-3+(\partial_{r}+\tfrac{2}{|x|})\zeta].

Then, we have

\displaystyle-{\partial}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}R\zeta_{R}\tfrac{x}{|x|}\cdot\operatorname{Im}(\bar{u}_{*}{\nabla u_{*}})dx=2\{\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{*})-\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(u_{*})\}.

Since
\zeta_{j,R}=0
for
|x|\leqslant R
(for
j=0,1,2,3
), we see from pre-compactness in
H^{1}
that there exists
R_{*}>0
such that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}{\partial}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}R_{*}\zeta_{R_{*}}\tfrac{x}{|x|}\cdot\operatorname{Im}(\bar{u}_{*}{\nabla u_{*}})dx\leqslant-\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u_{*}(t))\leqslant-\kappa_{*}<0.

However, this contradicts the fact that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}R_{*}\zeta_{R_{*}}\tfrac{x}{|x|}\cdot\operatorname{Im}(\bar{u}_{*}{\nabla u_{*}})dx\right\rvert\lesssim R_{*}M_{*}\sup_{t}\left\lVert\nabla u_{*}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}<\infty.

∎

In the rest of this section, we establish the remaining part of Theorem
1.1
in the focusing setting. In particular, focusing on the forward time direction, we will prove the following:

Proposition 3.8

(Growup result)
.

Let
\sigma=1
. Let
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfy

\mathbb{M}(u_{0})\leqslant\mu_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{V}(u_{0})<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mathbb{M}(u_{0})).

If
\mathbb{K}(u_{0})<0
and
\mathbb{H}_{0}(u_{0})\geqslant 1,
then the corresponding solution
u
to (
1.1
) satisfies

\lim_{t\uparrow T_{\max}}\left\lVert u(t)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}=\infty,

where
T_{\max}
is the maximal time of existence.

Proof.

We argue as in
[
1
]
. If
T_{\max}
is finite we obtain the result by the standard blowup criterion. We will prove that if
T_{\max}=\infty
,

\inf_{R>0}\limsup_{t\to\infty}\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|\nabla u(t)|^{2}dx=\infty,

which implies the result. Suppose instead that for some
R_{0}>0
, we have

h_{0}:=\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\int_{|x|\geqslant R_{0}}|\nabla u(t)|^{2}dx<\infty.

(3.20)

As
u\in C([0,\infty);H^{1})
, we see from (
2.5
) that there exists
\kappa>0
such that

\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u(t))\leqslant-\kappa\quad\text{for all}\quad t\geqslant 0.

(3.21)

We now define

W_{R}(x)=R^{2}\int_{0}^{|x|/R}\zeta(r)dr,

regarding
\zeta
as a function of
r
. We then have the virial identity

\displaystyle(W_{R},|u(t)|^{2})={}

\displaystyle(W_{R},|u_{0}|^{2})-2t\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}R\zeta_{R}\frac{x}{|x|}\cdot\operatorname{Im}(\bar{u}_{0}{\nabla u_{0}})dx

\displaystyle+4\left\{\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}(u(\sigma))d\sigma ds-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(u(\sigma))d\sigma ds\right\},

where
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}
is given by (
3.19
).

We first claim that there exists
m_{0}>0
such that if
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfies
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(v)\leqslant-\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa
,
\mathbb{M}(v)\leqslant\mathbb{M}(u_{0})
, and

\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|\nabla v|^{2}dx\leqslant h_{0}\quad\text{for some}\quad R\geqslant 1,

then

\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|v|^{2}dx\geqslant 2m_{0}

for the same
R
, where
h_{0}
is the constant given in (
3.20
).
Indeed, pick such
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Then, since
\zeta_{0,R}\geqslant 0
and
\zeta_{1,R}\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa\leqslant-\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(v)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\int|\zeta_{3,R}||v|^{4}dx+(\left\lVert\zeta_{2}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lVert x\cdot\nabla V\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}})\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|x|\geqslant R)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim{}

\displaystyle(\left\lVert\nabla v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|x|\geqslant R)}^{3}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}})\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|x|\geqslant R)}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle(h_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}+\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}})\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|x|\geqslant R)},

showing the desired lower bound.

We next claim that if
R
is sufficently large then

\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|u(t)|^{2}dx\leqslant m_{0}

(3.22)

for all
t\geqslant 0
. To this end, we first observe that if
R>0
is large then (
3.22
) is true at
t=0
. Now, suppose towards a contradiction that

T_{R}:=\sup\{T\geqslant 0\ |\ \eqref{e:growup_claim2}\text{ is true on }[0,T]\}<\infty.

As
u\in C([0,\infty);H^{1})
, we have
T_{R}>0
and

\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|u(T_{R})|^{2}dx=m_{0}.

(3.23)

Then, by the previous claim, we see that one of
\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(u(T_{R}))\leqslant-\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa
,
\mathbb{M}(u(T_{R}))\leqslant\mathbb{M}(u_{0})
, or
\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|\nabla u(T_{R})|^{2}dx\leqslant h_{0}
must fail. By mass conservation, the second of these is true. Furthermore, if
R\geqslant R_{0}
then the third item holds. Thus, if
R\geqslant R_{0}
,

\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(u(T_{R}))>-\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa.

Now, by the virial identity, we have

\displaystyle(W_{R},|u(T_{R})|^{2})\leq{}

\displaystyle(W_{R},|u_{0}|^{2})-2T_{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}R\zeta_{R}\frac{x}{|x|}\cdot\operatorname{Im}(\overline{u_{0}}{\nabla u_{0}})dx-\kappa T_{R}^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(W_{R},|u_{0}|^{2})-\kappa\left(T_{R}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}R\zeta_{R}\frac{x}{|x|}\cdot\operatorname{Im}(\overline{u_{0}}{\nabla u_{0}})dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}R\zeta_{R}\frac{x}{|x|}\cdot\operatorname{Im}(\overline{u_{0}}{\nabla u_{0}})dx\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle(W_{R},|u_{0}|^{2})+\tfrac{1}{2\kappa}\left\lVert\nabla u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\left\lVert R\zeta_{R}u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

We now claim that the right-hand side is
o(R^{2})
as
R\to\infty
. Indeed, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
R_{\varepsilon}
such that

\int_{|x|\geqslant R_{\varepsilon}}|u_{0}|^{2}dx\leqslant\varepsilon.

Furthermore, as
\left\lVert W_{R}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim R^{2}
and
\left\lVert\zeta_{R}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 3/2
, we have

\int_{|x|\geqslant R_{\varepsilon}}W_{R}|u_{0}|^{2}dx+\int_{|x|\geqslant R_{\varepsilon}}R^{2}\zeta_{R}^{2}|u_{0}|^{2}dx\lesssim\varepsilon R^{2}.

Finally, if
R\geqslant R_{\varepsilon}
then
W_{R}=|x|^{2}
and
R\zeta_{R}=|x|
for
|x|\leqslant R_{\varepsilon}
and hence

\int_{|x|\leqslant R_{\varepsilon}}W_{R}|u_{0}|^{2}dx+\int_{|x|\leqslant R_{\varepsilon}}R^{2}\zeta_{R}^{2}|u_{0}|^{2}dx\lesssim R_{\varepsilon}^{2}\left\lVert u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Combining these two estimate, we obtain the claim.

Choosing
R
sufficiently large, we therefore have that

(W_{R},|u(T_{R})|^{2})\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}m_{0}R^{2}.

Consequently,

R^{2}\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|u(T_{R})|^{2}dx\leqslant(W_{R},|u(T_{R})|^{2})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}m_{0}R^{2},

which contradicts (
3.23
). Thus, we obtain (
3.22
) for all
t\geq 0
.

Combining above two claims, we see that there exists
R>0
such that

\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\int_{|x|\geqslant R}|u(t)|^{2}dx\leqslant m_{0},\quad\inf_{t\geqslant 0}\mathbb{K}_{V,2}^{R}(u(t))\geqslant-\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa.

However, by the virial identity, one then obtains

0\leqslant(W_{R},|u(t)|^{2})\leqslant C(u_{0})t-\kappa t^{2}

for all
t\geqslant 0
, which yields a contradiction for sufficiently large
t
.
∎

So far, we have restricted our attention primarily to the focusing case of (
1.1
). Straightforward modifications suffice to treat the defocusing case, as well. In particular, due to the absence of excited solitons (cf. Conjecture
2.1
), the condition
\mathbb{E}_{V}(u_{0})<\mathscr{E}_{1}(\mathbb{M}(u_{0}))
does not impose any restriction on the size of the energy. Moreover, the condition (
1.4
) cannot be satisfied in the defocusing case (cf. Proposition
2.3
); thus, in the defocusing case, only the scattering alternative occurs.

4.
Strichartz estimates and stability for the linearized equation

In the previous section, we saw that any small mass solution to (
1.1
) admits a decomposition of the form
u=\Phi[z]+R[z]\xi
. In this section, our interest is in the equation satisfied by
\xi(t)
, as well as the underlying linear part of this equation.

We introduce the following set of functions from
[
19
]
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathrm{SBC}
denote the set of continuous functions
z(t):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfying either of the following:

•

z(t)
arises from a global solution
u(t)
to (
1.1
) such that

\mathbb{M}(u)\leqslant\mu_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}_{0}(u(t))\lesssim\mu^{-1}

via the decomposition
u(t)=\Phi[z(t)]+\eta(t)
.

•

z(t)
arises from a solution
u(t)
to (
1.1
) defined on an interval
I
via the decomposition
u(t)=\Phi[z(t)]+\eta(t)
. Outside of
I
,
z(t)
is constant.

An element
z(t)
of
\mathrm{SBC}
is continuously differentiable on
\mathbb{R}
with at most two exceptional points and obeys the bounds

\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\mu_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert\dot{z}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 1.

Remark 4.2
.

By the Arzelá–Ascoli Theorem, any sequence
\{z_{n}\}_{n}\subset\mathrm{SBC}
has a subsequence that converges locally uniformly to a continuous bounded function,
z_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}
. Using the fact that a weak limit of bounded solutions to (
1.1
) is again a solution, it follows that
z_{\infty}\in\mathrm{SBC}
.

We next introduce the solution operator for the linearized equation. Recall that
B[z]:=P_{c}\tilde{B}[z]R[z]
, where

\tilde{B}[z]f=2|\Phi[z]|^{2}f+\Phi[z]^{2}\bar{f},

with
z\mapsto\Phi[z]
parametrizing the ground states and
R[z]
giving the inverse of
P_{c}|_{P_{c}[z]H^{1}}
(cf. Proposition
3.2
and the subsequent discussion).

Definition 4.3
.

For
z\in\mathrm{SBC}
, we define
\mathcal{L}(t,s;z):P_{c}H^{1}\to P_{c}H^{1}
as follows:
